<a id="pm-s1"></a>
## 1. Abstract and result contract

> We prove that a nonzero real Laplace eigenfunction with eigenvalue <i>λ</i> &gt; 0 on a fixed smooth closed connected Riemannian surface has nodal length at most $`C\sqrt\lambda`$. Together with the known lower bound, this proves Yau's conjecture in this setting.

- **claim-nodal-length-main** (theorem, [source](#stmt-thm-main)): Let $(M,g)$ be a closed connected $C^\infty$ Riemannian surface. There is a finite constant $C=C(M,g)$ such that every nonzero real Laplace eigenfunction satisfying $$-\Delta_g u=\lambda u,\qquad \lambda>0,$$ obeys $$\mathcal H_g^1(Z_u)\le C\sqrt\lambda.$$ Here $\mathcal H_g^1$ is Hausdorff measure for the Riemannian distance. The same constant applies to every eigenfunction in every positive eigenspace.
  - Explanation: On any fixed closed connected smooth Riemannian surface the nodal length of every nonzero real eigenfunction is bounded by a surface-dependent constant times the square root of the eigenvalue, uniformly over all eigenspaces. Combined with the known lower bound this settles Yau's conjecture for surfaces.
- **claim-profile-extraction** (theorem, [source](#stmt-lem-profile-extraction)): Let $X$ be an open subset of $\mathbb R^2$ or a compact smooth surface. Suppose that $S_j\to\infty$ and that $\mu_j$ are nonnegative Borel measures on $X$ with $\mu_j(X)\le 1$. There are a subsequence and an upper semicontinuous function $V\colon X\to[-\infty,0]$ such that, for every compact $F\subset X$ and every open $G\subset X$, $$\label{eq:profile-bounds} \limsup_{j\to\infty}\frac{\log\mu_j(F)}{2S_j}\le\sup_F V, \qquad \liminf_{j\to\infty}\frac{\log\mu_j(G)}{2S_j}\ge\sup_G V.$$ Here $\log 0=\sup\varnothing=-\infty$. More generally, if continuous real functions $f_j$ converge locally uniformly to $f$, then $$\begin{aligned} \limsup_{j\to\infty}\frac{1}{2S_j} \log\int_F e^{2S_j f_j}\,d\mu_j&\le\sup_F(V+f),\\ \liminf_{j\to\infty}\frac{1}{2S_j} \log\int_G e^{2S_j f_j}\,d\mu_j&\ge\sup_G(V+f). \end{aligned}$$ The same subsequence works for all these sets and weights.
  - Explanation: From measures of total mass at most one on exponential scales one extracts a subsequence and an upper semicontinuous logarithmic profile that controls masses of compact and open sets, also under continuous exponential weights.
- **claim-subharmonic-profile** (theorem, [source](#stmt-thm-subharmonic-profile)): On a fixed planar disk $\Omega$, let real smooth functions $U_j,p_j$ satisfy $$\Delta U_j+K_j^2p_jU_j=0,$$ where $K_j\ge0$. Let $S_j>0$, $B_j\in\mathbb R^2$, and $D_j=S_j+\|B_j\|$. Assume $$\label{eq:profile-hypotheses} \begin{gathered} S_j\longrightarrow\infty,\qquad \frac{K_j}{D_j}\longrightarrow0,\\ \sup_j\\|p_j\\|_\infty<\infty,\qquad \frac{K_j^2\\|\nabla p_j\\|_\infty}{S_jD_j}\longrightarrow0. \end{gathered}$$ Local versions of the coefficient assumptions on relatively compact subdisks suffice. For positive constants $c_j$, suppose the measures $$d\mu_j=c_j e^{-2B_j\cdot x}\|U_j(x)\|^2\,dx$$ have mass at most one on $\Omega$. Every upper semicontinuous logarithmic profile with the compact, open, and weighted bounds of Lemma <a href="#lem:profile-extraction" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:profile-extraction">2</a> is subharmonic on $\Omega$, with the identically $-\infty$ function allowed.
  - Explanation: Logarithmic profiles of solutions of perturbed Helmholtz equations are subharmonic even when the exponential weight carries an arbitrarily large affine tilt, provided the stated parameter and coefficient conditions hold.
- **claim-local-carleman** (theorem, [source](#stmt-lem-carleman)): Assume the coefficient and parameter hypotheses of Theorem <a href="#thm:subharmonic-profile" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:subharmonic-profile">3</a>. After passing to a subsequence, write $$S_j/D_j\to d,\qquad B_j/D_j\to b, \qquad d\ge0,\quad d+\|b\|=1.$$ Let $\chi$ be a real smooth function near $x_0\in\Omega$ such that $\Delta\chi(x_0)<0$ and $b+d\nabla\chi(x_0)\ne0$. Put $T_j(x)=-B_j\cdot x-S_j\chi(x)$. There are a neighborhood $\Omega_0$ of $x_0$, a constant $c>0$, and an index $j_0$ such that $$\label{eq:carleman} \\|e^{T_j}(\Delta+K_j^2p_j)w\\|_2 \ge c\sqrt{S_j}\,D_j\,\\|e^{T_j}w\\|_2$$ for all $j\ge j_0$ and all real $w\in C_c^\infty(\Omega_0)$.
  - Explanation: A local Carleman inequality with a tilted weight whose gradient is nonzero and whose Laplacian is negative at the base point. In dimension two the commutator sees the full trace of the weight Hessian, which gives the gain.
- **claim-exceptional-gradient** (theorem, [source](#stmt-lem-exceptional-gradient)): Let $W\colon\Omega\to[-\infty,\infty)$ be upper semicontinuous and locally bounded above on a connected planar domain. Suppose every smooth function $\psi$ touching $W$ from above at a finite point $x_0$, with $\nabla\psi(x_0)\ne0$, satisfies $\Delta\psi(x_0)\ge0$. Then $W$ is subharmonic, with the identically $-\infty$ function allowed.
  - Explanation: An upper semicontinuous function that satisfies the upper-test criterion at all points with nonzero test gradient is subharmonic, so the zero-gradient exception can be dropped.
- **claim-initial-growth** (theorem, [source](#stmt-prop-initial-growth)): Let $(M,g)$ be a fixed smooth closed connected surface, and let $G\subset M$ be a fixed nonempty open coordinate disk. There is a finite constant $C=C(M,g,G)$ such that every nonzero real eigenfunction $-\Delta_g u=k^2u$ with $k>0$ satisfies $\\|u\\|_{L^2(G)}>0$ and $$\label{eq:initial-growth} \log\frac{\\|u\\|_{L^2(M)}}{\\|u\\|_{L^2(G)}}\le Ck.$$ Both norms use Riemannian volume. The constant is uniform over all eigenfunctions in every eigenspace.
  - Explanation: Every nonzero eigenfunction is nonvanishing in a fixed coordinate disk, and the logarithmic ratio of its global to local norm is at most linear in the frequency, uniformly over all eigenspaces.
- **claim-potential-decomposition** (theorem, [source](#stmt-lem-potential-decomposition)): Let $V$ be subharmonic on $\mathbb D_R$, with $$V\le0, \qquad \sup_{\overline{\mathbb D_1}}V\ge-1.$$ Then $\norm{V}_{L^1(\mathbb D_8)}$ and the mass of its Riesz measure $(2\pi)^{-1}\Delta V$ on $\mathbb D_5$ are uniformly bounded. There are a nonnegative measure $\nu$ on $\mathbb D_4$, of uniformly bounded mass, and a harmonic function $h$ on $\mathbb D_3$ such that $$V(x)=h(x)+\int_{\mathbb D_4}\log\abs{x-z}\,d\nu(z), \qquad x\in\mathbb D_3.$$ The first and second derivatives of $h$ on $\mathbb D_2$ are uniformly bounded. All bounds are independent of $V$, and the representation holds for the pointwise subharmonic representatives, including values equal to $-\infty$.
  - Explanation: A normalized subharmonic function splits into a harmonic part with bounded derivatives plus a logarithmic potential of a measure of uniformly bounded mass, with bounds independent of the function.
- **claim-affine-profile** (theorem, [source](#stmt-lem-affine-profile)): Let $V$, $h$, and $\nu$ be as in Lemma <a href="#lem:potential-decomposition" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:potential-decomposition">8</a>. Subdivide the unit square $[-1/2,1/2]^2$ into $A^2$ squares, where $A>10R$, put $\rho=A^{-1}$, and denote their centers by $y$. Define $$c_y=\nabla h(y) +\int_{\substack{z\in\mathbb D_4\\\abs{z-y}>2R\rho}} \frac{y-z}{\abs{y-z}^2}\,d\nu(z).$$ Then $$\begin{aligned} &\sup_{\overline{\mathbb D_{R\rho}(y)}}(V(x)-c_y\cdot x) -\sup_{\mathbb D_\rho(y)}(V(x)-c_y\cdot x)\\ &\hspace{1cm}\le C\left(\rho^2+\int_{\mathbb D_4} \min\left\{1,\frac{\rho^2}{\abs{z-y}^2}\right\}\,d\nu(z) \right). \end{aligned} \label{eq:affine-error}$$ At $z=y$ the minimum in this formula is interpreted as one. Moreover, $$\mathop{\mathrm{avg}}_y\abs{c_y}\le C, \qquad \mathop{\mathrm{avg}}_y\left[\text{right-hand side of \eqref{eq:affine-error}}\right] \le C\rho^2(1+\abs{\log\rho}).$$ The constants may depend on $R$, but are independent of $A$ and $V$.
  - Explanation: Subtracting a suitable affine function at each grid centre makes the excess of the profile between concentric disks small on average over the grid, while the average correction slope stays bounded.
- **claim-one-subdivision-step** (theorem, [source](#stmt-prop-subdivision)): There are an integer $A>10R$ and constants $C_0,C_1>0$, depending only on the fixed coordinate coefficient bounds and $R$, with the following property. Let $Q$ be an admissible square of side $s\le1$, let $K=ks>0$, and let $b\in\mathbb R^2$ and $S>0$. Put $B=Kb$. If $$S\ge C_0,\qquad E(Q,b)\le S,\qquad \frac{K}{S+\abs B}\le\frac1{C_0},\qquad \frac{K^2s}{S(S+\abs B)}\le\frac1{C_0}, \label{eq:subdivision-hypotheses}$$ then real vectors $b_q$ can be chosen for the children $q\prec Q$ so that $$\mathop{\mathrm{avg}}_{q\prec Q}\abs{b_q-b}\le C_1\frac SK, \qquad \mathop{\mathrm{avg}}_{q\prec Q}m(q,b_q)\le\frac12\frac SK. \label{eq:subdivision-estimate}$$ The constants are independent of the eigenfunction, the scale, the depth of a tree, and any lower bound for $K$.
  - Explanation: Under the stated smallness conditions one subdivision step with suitably chosen child tilts halves the average normalized growth while the average tilt change is controlled by the scaled bound, with constants independent of the eigenfunction and of any lower bound for the frequency-side product.
- **claim-one-step-descent** (theorem, [source](#stmt-lem-descent)): There is a fixed $L>0$ such that, at any nonterminal square $Q$, if $N(Q)>L$ or $\abs{b}>L$, one can assign a tilt $b_q$ to each child $q$ so that, writing $m=m(Q,b)$, $m'=m(q,b_q)$, and $b'=b_q$, one has $$\label{eq:descent} \begin{gathered} \mathbb E\bigl(\abs{b'-b}\mid Q,b\bigr)\le C_1(m+f_Q), \qquad \mathbb E\bigl(m'\mid Q,b\bigr)\le \tfrac12(m+f_Q),\\ f_Q=\frac{C_0}{K_Q}+s(Q). \end{gathered}$$ Moreover, if $Q_0=I,Q_1,\ldots,Q_d$ is any path from $I$ to the terminal level, then $$\label{eq:floors} \sum_{i=0}^{d}f_{Q_i} \le \frac{A}{A-1} \left(\frac{C_0}{a_0}+s(I)\right).$$
  - Explanation: Whenever the untilted growth or the tilt exceeds a fixed threshold, children tilts exist that contract the expected normalized growth up to a floor term, and the floors sum to a bounded quantity along every path.
- **claim-stopped-descent** (theorem, [source](#stmt-lem-stopped-descent)): Start at $I$ with a prescribed tilt $b_I$. Whenever a step is made, choose the child tilts by Lemma <a href="#lem:descent" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:descent">11</a>, and suppose that its applicability condition holds at every square from which the procedure continues. Stop at a time $T$, no later than the terminal level. Write $m_i=m(Q_i,b_i)$ for the visited values, including the last value $m_T$. There is a constant $C_{\mathrm{st}}$, independent of the depth and of the stopping rule, such that $$\label{eq:stopped-descent} \mathbb E\left[ \sum_{i=0}^{T}m_i +\sum_{i=0}^{T-1}\abs{b_{i+1}-b_i} \right] \le C_{\mathrm{st}}\bigl(m(I,b_I)+1\bigr).$$ The second sum includes the step producing the last square, even if that square causes the procedure to stop.
  - Explanation: Along descents that may stop at any time depending on visited data, the expected accumulated growth plus tilt variation is bounded in terms of the initial growth, independently of depth and stopping rule.
- **claim-large-slope-large-growth** (theorem, [source](#stmt-lem-large-slope)): For the fixed $L$ in Lemma <a href="#lem:descent" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:descent">11</a>, there are constants $B_*\ge1$ and $\eta_*>0$ such that every admissible square with $s(Q)\le1$ and $K_Q\ge a_0$ satisfies $$\label{eq:large-slope} \abs{b}\ge B_*, \qquad m(Q,b)\le\eta_*\abs{b} \quad\Longrightarrow\quad N(Q)>L.$$
  - Explanation: A sufficiently large tilt whose normalized residual growth is small relative to the tilt forces the untilted growth to exceed the descent threshold.
- **claim-stability-large-growth** (theorem, [source](#stmt-prop-stability)): There are constants $P\ge1$ and $\sigma>0$ with the following property. Suppose a square $I$ and a tilt $b_I$ satisfy $$\label{eq:stability-hypotheses} \abs{b_I}\ge P,\qquad m(I,b_I)\le\sigma\abs{b_I}.$$ Then at least half the paths from $I$ to the terminal level satisfy $N(Q)>L$ at every visited square $Q$, including $I$ and the terminal square. Equivalently, these paths occupy a measurable set of area at least $\abs{I}/2$.
  - Explanation: If a square starts with large tilt and small residual growth, at least half of the paths to the terminal level keep untilted growth above the threshold at every visited square.
- **claim-entry-growth-bound** (theorem, [source](#stmt-lem-entry-bound)): For all sufficiently large fixed $H$, every entry $Q$ and its grid parent $Q^p$ satisfy $$\label{eq:entry-bound} N(Q^p)\le H, \qquad H<N(Q)\le A^2\bigl[\tfrac12+(R+1)C_1\bigr] \left(H+\frac{C_0}{a_0}\right).$$
  - Explanation: At an entry the parent has growth at most the threshold, while the entry itself has growth above the threshold but bounded by an explicit multiple of it.
- **claim-persistence-near-entry** (theorem, [source](#stmt-prop-persistence)): There are a finite threshold $H$, a number $\delta>0$, and an integer $J\ge0$ such that every entry $Q$ admits a measurable set $Y_Q\subset D_*Q$, where $D_*=20A$, with $$\label{eq:persistence} \begin{split} \|Y_Q\|&\ge\delta\|Q\|,\\ \#\{F\in\mathcal E:\ s(F)<s(Q),\ y\in F\}&\le J \qquad\text{for almost every }y\in Y_Q. \end{split}$$ Only entries of the given tree are counted, including when $Y_Q$ extends outside the root.
  - Explanation: Near every entry there is a witness set of proportional area, possibly outside the entry square, on which almost every point lies in at most a bounded number of strictly finer entries.
- **claim-packing-witnesses** (theorem, [source](#stmt-lem-packing)): Let $\mathcal F$ be a finite family of squares in the $A$-adic subdivision tree of a square $Q_0$, where $A\ge2$. Suppose there are fixed $D\ge1$, $\delta>0$, and an integer $J\ge0$ such that every $Q\in\mathcal F$ admits a measurable set $Y_Q\subset DQ$ satisfying $$\|Y_Q\|\ge\delta\|Q\|, \qquad \#\{F\in\mathcal F:s(F)<s(Q),\ y\in F\}\le J \quad\text{for almost every }y\in Y_Q.$$ Then $$\label{eq:packing} \sum_{Q\in\mathcal F}\|Q\| \le C(A,D,\delta,J)\|Q_0\|.$$
  - Explanation: A finite family of squares in an adic subdivision tree whose members have proportional dilated witness sets with bounded finer overlap has total area bounded by a constant multiple of the root area. The proof counts ancestry-versus-membership mismatches, which live in boundary collars.
- **claim-bounded-mean-growth** (theorem, [source](#stmt-thm-mean-growth)): For the finite trees considered in this section, let $\mathcal T_{\mathrm{term}}$ be the collection of terminal squares and let $s_{\mathrm{term}}$ be their common side length. There is a constant $C$, independent of the eigenfunction and the number of levels, such that $$\label{eq:mean-growth} \mathop{\mathrm{avg}}_{q\in\mathcal T_{\mathrm{term}}}N(q)\le C, \qquad \mathop{\mathrm{avg}}_{q\in\mathcal T_{\mathrm{term}}}E(q,0) \le Ck s_{\mathrm{term}}.$$
  - Explanation: The average untilted growth over terminal squares is bounded independently of the eigenfunction and the number of levels, so the average affine excess is at most a constant times the frequency-side product.
- **claim-roy-fortin-local-length** (theorem, [source](#stmt-thm-local-length)): There are constants $\varepsilon_0>0$ and $C>0$ with the following property. Let $F$ be a nonzero real solution of $$\Delta F+qF=0\quad\text{on }\mathbb D_3, \qquad q\in C^\infty(\mathbb D_3),\quad \norm{q}_\infty<\varepsilon_0.$$ If $$\beta=\log\frac{\norm{F}_{L^\infty(\mathbb D_{5/2})}} {\norm{F}_{L^\infty(\mathbb D_{1/4})}},$$ then $$\mathcal H^1(Z_F\cap\mathbb D_{1/60})\le C\max\{1,\beta\}.$$
  - Explanation: The cited planar theorem bounds the nodal length of a small-potential solution in a small disk by the logarithmic ratio of supremum norms on two concentric disks.
- **claim-wavelength-length** (theorem, [source](#stmt-lem-wavelength-length)): Fix the coefficient bounds and coordinate neighborhood from Section <a href="#sec:subdivision" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:subdivision">3</a>. There is an $\varepsilon>0$ such that, for every admissible square $I$ of side $s$ with $ks\le\varepsilon$, $$\label{eq:wavelength-length} \mathcal H^1(I\cap Z_u)\le Cs\bigl(1+E(I,0)\bigr).$$ The constants are independent of $u$, $k$, and $s$.
  - Explanation: On an admissible square at wavelength scale the nodal length is at most the side times one plus the untilted logarithmic growth, with constants independent of the eigenfunction, frequency and side.

```json
      "exact_statement": "Let $(M,g)$ be a closed connected $C^\\infty$ Riemannian surface.       "conclusion": {
        "anchor": "stmt-lem-profile-extraction",
        "exact_quote": "Let $X$ be an open subset of $\\mathbb R^2$ or a compact smooth surface. Suppose that $S_j\\to\\infty$ and that $\\mu_j$ are nonnegative Borel measures on $X$ with $\\mu_j(X)\\le 1$. There are a subsequence and an upper semicontinuous function $V\\colon X\\to[-\\infty,0]$ such that, for every compact $F\\subset X$ and every open $G\\subset X$, $$\\label{eq:profile-bounds}\n \\limsup_{j\\to\\infty}\\frac{\\log\\mu_j(F)}{2S_j}\\le\\sup_F V,\n \\qquad\n \\liminf_{j\\to\\infty}\\frac{\\log\\mu_j(G)}{2S_j}\\ge\\sup_G V.$$ Here $\\log 0=\\sup\\varnothing=-\\infty$. More generally, if continuous real functions $f_j$ converge locally uniformly to $f$, then $$\\begin{aligned}\n \\limsup_{j\\to\\infty}\\frac{1}{2S_j}\n       \\log\\int_F e^{2S_j f_j}\\,d\\mu_j&\\le\\sup_F(V+f),\\\\\n \\liminf_{j\\to\\infty}\\frac{1}{2S_j}\n       \\log\\int_G e^{2S_j f_j}\\,d\\mu_j&\\ge\\sup_G(V+f).\n \\end{aligned}$$ The same subsequence works for all these sets and weights.",
        "source_id": "PM-350.1"
      },
      "evidence": [
        {

      "conclusion": {
        "anchor": "stmt-thm-subharmonic-profile",
        "exact_quote": "On a fixed planar disk $\\Omega$, let real smooth functions $U_j,p_j$ satisfy $$\\Delta U_j+K_j^2p_jU_j=0,$$ where $K_j\\ge0$. Let $S_j>0$, $B_j\\in\\mathbb R^2$, and $D_j=S_j+|B_j|$. Assume $$\\label{eq:profile-hypotheses}\n \\begin{gathered}\n S_j\\longrightarrow\\infty,\\qquad\n \\frac{K_j}{D_j}\\longrightarrow0,\\\\\n \\sup_j\\|p_j\\|_\\infty<\\infty,\\qquad\n \\frac{K_j^2\\|\\nabla p_j\\|_\\infty}{S_jD_j}\\longrightarrow0.\n \\end{gathered}$$ Local versions of the coefficient assumptions on relatively compact subdisks suffice. For positive constants $c_j$, suppose the measures $$d\\mu_j=c_j e^{-2B_j\\cdot x}|U_j(x)|^2\\,dx$$ have mass at most one on $\\Omega$. Every upper semicontinuous logarithmic profile with the compact, open, and weighted bounds of Lemma <a href=\"#lem:profile-extraction\" data-reference-type=\"ref\" data-reference=\"lem:profile-extraction\">2</a> is subharmonic on $\\Omega$, with the identically $-\\infty$ function allowed.",
        "source_id": "PM-350.1"
      },
      "evidence": [

        "anchor": "stmt-lem-carleman",
        "exact_quote": "Assume the coefficient and parameter hypotheses of Theorem <a href=\"#thm:subharmonic-profile\" data-reference-type=\"ref\" data-reference=\"thm:subharmonic-profile\">3</a>. After passing to a subsequence, write $$S_j/D_j\\to d,\\qquad B_j/D_j\\to b,\n \\qquad d\\ge0,\\quad d+|b|=1.$$ Let $\\chi$ be a real smooth function near $x_0\\in\\Omega$ such that $\\Delta\\chi(x_0)<0$ and $b+d\\nabla\\chi(x_0)\\ne0$. Put $T_j(x)=-B_j\\cdot x-S_j\\chi(x)$. There are a neighborhood $\\Omega_0$ of $x_0$, a constant $c>0$, and an index $j_0$ such that $$\\label{eq:carleman}\n \\|e^{T_j}(\\Delta+K_j^2p_j)w\\|_2\n \\ge c\\sqrt{S_j}\\,D_j\\,\\|e^{T_j}w\\|_2$$ for all $j\\ge j_0$ and all real $w\\in C_c^\\infty(\\Omega_0)$.",

      },

        "exact_quote": "Let $W\\colon\\Omega\\to[-\\infty,\\infty)$ be upper semicontinuous and locally bounded above on a connected planar domain.         "anchor": "stmt-lem-potential-decomposition",
        "exact_quote": "Let $V$ be subharmonic on $\\mathbb D_R$, with $$V\\le0,\n \\qquad \\sup_{\\overline{\\mathbb D_1}}V\\ge-1.$$ Then $\\norm{V}_{L^1(\\mathbb D_8)}$ and the mass of its Riesz measure $(2\\pi)^{-1}\\Delta V$ on $\\mathbb D_5$ are uniformly bounded. There are a nonnegative measure $\\nu$ on $\\mathbb D_4$, of uniformly bounded mass, and a harmonic function $h$ on $\\mathbb D_3$ such that $$V(x)=h(x)+\\int_{\\mathbb D_4}\\log\\abs{x-z}\\,d\\nu(z),\n \\qquad x\\in\\mathbb D_3.$$ The first and second derivatives of $h$ on $\\mathbb D_2$ are uniformly bounded.       "conclusion": {
        "anchor": "stmt-lem-affine-profile",
        "exact_quote": "Let $V$, $h$, and $\\nu$ be as in Lemma <a href=\"#lem:potential-decomposition\" data-reference-type=\"ref\" data-reference=\"lem:potential-decomposition\">8</a>. Subdivide the unit square $[-1/2,1/2]^2$ into $A^2$ squares, where $A>10R$, put $\\rho=A^{-1}$, and denote their centers by $y$. Define $$c_y=\\nabla h(y)\n   +\\int_{\\substack{z\\in\\mathbb D_4\\\\\\abs{z-y}>2R\\rho}}\n        \\frac{y-z}{\\abs{y-z}^2}\\,d\\nu(z).$$ Then $$\\begin{aligned}\n &\\sup_{\\overline{\\mathbb D_{R\\rho}(y)}}(V(x)-c_y\\cdot x)\n     -\\sup_{\\mathbb D_\\rho(y)}(V(x)-c_y\\cdot x)\\\\\n &\\hspace{1cm}\\le\n C\\left(\\rho^2+\\int_{\\mathbb D_4}\n      \\min\\left\\{1,\\frac{\\rho^2}{\\abs{z-y}^2}\\right\\}\\,d\\nu(z)\n   \\right).\n\\end{aligned}\n\\label{eq:affine-error}$$ At $z=y$ the minimum in this formula is interpreted as one. Moreover, $$\\mathop{\\mathrm{avg}}_y\\abs{c_y}\\le C,\n \\qquad\n \\mathop{\\mathrm{avg}}_y\\left[\\text{right-hand side of \\eqref{eq:affine-error}}\\right]\n \\le C\\rho^2(1+\\abs{\\log\\rho}).$$ The constants may depend on $R$, but are independent of $A$ and $V$.",
        "source_id": "PM-350.1"
      },
      "evidence": [
        {
          "anchor": "stmt-lem-affine-profile",
          "exact_quote": "Let $V$, $h$, and $\\nu$ be as in Lemma <a href=\"#lem:potential-decomposition\" data-reference-type=\"ref\" data-reference=\"lem:potential-decomposition\">8</a>. Subdivide the unit square $[-1/2,1/2]^2$ into $A^2$ squares, where $A>10R$, put $\\rho=A^{-1}$, and denote their centers by $y$. Define $$c_y=\\nabla h(y)\n   +\\int_{\\substack{z\\in\\mathbb D_4\\\\\\abs{z-y}>2R\\rho}}\n        \\frac{y-z}{\\abs{y-z}^2}\\,d\\nu(z).$$ Then $$\\begin{aligned}\n &\\sup_{\\overline{\\mathbb D_{R\\rho}(y)}}(V(x)-c_y\\cdot x)\n     -\\sup_{\\mathbb D_\\rho(y)}(V(x)-c_y\\cdot x)\\\\\n &\\hspace{1cm}\\le\n C\\left(\\rho^2+\\int_{\\mathbb D_4}\n      \\min\\left\\{1,\\frac{\\rho^2}{\\abs{z-y}^2}\\right\\}\\,d\\nu(z)\n   \\right).\n\\end{aligned}\n\\label{eq:affine-error}$$ At $z=y$ the minimum in this formula is interpreted as one. Moreover, $$\\mathop{\\mathrm{avg}}_y\\abs{c_y}\\le C,\n \\qquad\n \\mathop{\\mathrm{avg}}_y\\left[\\text{right-hand side of \\eqref{eq:affine-error}}\\right]\n \\le C\\rho^2(1+\\abs{\\log\\rho}).$$ The constants may depend on $R$, but are independent of $A$ and $V$.",
          "source_id": "PM-350.1"
        }
      ],
      "exact_statement": "Let $V$, $h$, and $\\nu$ be as in Lemma <a href=\"#lem:potential-decomposition\" data-reference-type=\"ref\" data-reference=\"lem:potential-decomposition\">8</a>. Subdivide the unit square $[-1/2,1/2]^2$ into $A^2$ squares, where $A>10R$, put $\\rho=A^{-1}$, and denote their centers by $y$. Define $$c_y=\\nabla h(y)\n   +\\int_{\\substack{z\\in\\mathbb D_4\\\\\\abs{z-y}>2R\\rho}}\n        \\frac{y-z}{\\abs{y-z}^2}\\,d\\nu(z).$$ Then $$\\begin{aligned}\n &\\sup_{\\overline{\\mathbb D_{R\\rho}(y)}}(V(x)-c_y\\cdot x)\n     -\\sup_{\\mathbb D_\\rho(y)}(V(x)-c_y\\cdot x)\\\\\n &\\hspace{1cm}\\le\n C\\left(\\rho^2+\\int_{\\mathbb D_4}\n      \\min\\left\\{1,\\frac{\\rho^2}{\\abs{z-y}^2}\\right\\}\\,d\\nu(z)\n   \\right).\n\\end{aligned}\n\\label{eq:affine-error}$$ At $z=y$ the minimum in this formula is interpreted as one. Moreover, $$\\mathop{\\mathrm{avg}}_y\\abs{c_y}\\le C,\n \\qquad\n \\mathop{\\mathrm{avg}}_y\\left[\\text{right-hand side of \\eqref{eq:affine-error}}\\right]\n \\le C\\rho^2(1+\\abs{\\log\\rho}).$$ The constants may depend on $R$, but are independent of $A$ and $V$.",

        "anchor": "stmt-prop-subdivision",
        "exact_quote": "There are an integer $A>10R$ and constants $C_0,C_1>0$, depending only on the fixed coordinate coefficient bounds and $R$, with the following property. Let $Q$ be an admissible square of side $s\\le1$, let $K=ks>0$, and let $b\\in\\mathbb R^2$ and $S>0$. Put $B=Kb$. If $$S\\ge C_0,\\qquad E(Q,b)\\le S,\\qquad\n \\frac{K}{S+\\abs B}\\le\\frac1{C_0},\\qquad\n \\frac{K^2s}{S(S+\\abs B)}\\le\\frac1{C_0},\n\\label{eq:subdivision-hypotheses}$$ then real vectors $b_q$ can be chosen for the children $q\\prec Q$ so that $$\\mathop{\\mathrm{avg}}_{q\\prec Q}\\abs{b_q-b}\\le C_1\\frac SK,\n \\qquad\n \\mathop{\\mathrm{avg}}_{q\\prec Q}m(q,b_q)\\le\\frac12\\frac SK.\n\\label{eq:subdivision-estimate}$$ The constants are independent of the eigenfunction, the scale, the depth of a tree, and any lower bound for $K$.",

    {
      "conclusion": {
        "anchor": "stmt-lem-descent",
        "exact_quote": "There is a fixed $L>0$ such that, at any nonterminal square $Q$, if $N(Q)>L$ or $\\abs{b}>L$, one can assign a tilt $b_q$ to each child $q$ so that, writing $m=m(Q,b)$, $m'=m(q,b_q)$, and $b'=b_q$, one has $$\\label{eq:descent}\n \\begin{gathered}\n \\mathbb E\\bigl(\\abs{b'-b}\\mid Q,b\\bigr)\\le C_1(m+f_Q),\n \\qquad\n \\mathbb E\\bigl(m'\\mid Q,b\\bigr)\\le \\tfrac12(m+f_Q),\\\\\n f_Q=\\frac{C_0}{K_Q}+s(Q).\n \\end{gathered}$$ Moreover, if $Q_0=I,Q_1,\\ldots,Q_d$ is any path from $I$ to the terminal level, then $$\\label{eq:floors}\n \\sum_{i=0}^{d}f_{Q_i}\n \\le \\frac{A}{A-1}\n       \\left(\\frac{C_0}{a_0}+s(I)\\right).$$",
        "source_id": "PM-350.1"
      },
      "evidence": [
        {
          "anchor": "stmt-lem-descent",
          "exact_quote": "There is a fixed $L>0$ such that, at any nonterminal square $Q$, if $N(Q)>L$ or $\\abs{b}>L$, one can assign a tilt $b_q$ to each child $q$ so that, writing $m=m(Q,b)$, $m'=m(q,b_q)$, and $b'=b_q$, one has $$\\label{eq:descent}\n \\begin{gathered}\n \\mathbb E\\bigl(\\abs{b'-b}\\mid Q,b\\bigr)\\le C_1(m+f_Q),\n \\qquad\n \\mathbb E\\bigl(m'\\mid Q,b\\bigr)\\le \\tfrac12(m+f_Q),\\\\\n f_Q=\\frac{C_0}{K_Q}+s(Q).\n \\end{gathered}$$ Moreover, if $Q_0=I,Q_1,\\ldots,Q_d$ is any path from $I$ to the terminal level, then $$\\label{eq:floors}\n \\sum_{i=0}^{d}f_{Q_i}\n \\le \\frac{A}{A-1}\n       \\left(\\frac{C_0}{a_0}+s(I)\\right).$$",
          "source_id": "PM-350.1"
        }
      ],
      "exact_statement": "There is a fixed $L>0$ such that, at any nonterminal square $Q$, if $N(Q)>L$ or $\\abs{b}>L$, one can assign a tilt $b_q$ to each child $q$ so that, writing $m=m(Q,b)$, $m'=m(q,b_q)$, and $b'=b_q$, one has $$\\label{eq:descent}\n \\begin{gathered}\n \\mathbb E\\bigl(\\abs{b'-b}\\mid Q,b\\bigr)\\le C_1(m+f_Q),\n \\qquad\n \\mathbb E\\bigl(m'\\mid Q,b\\bigr)\\le \\tfrac12(m+f_Q),\\\\\n f_Q=\\frac{C_0}{K_Q}+s(Q).\n \\end{gathered}$$ Moreover, if $Q_0=I,Q_1,\\ldots,Q_d$ is any path from $I$ to the terminal level, then $$\\label{eq:floors}\n \\sum_{i=0}^{d}f_{Q_i}\n \\le \\frac{A}{A-1}\n       \\left(\\frac{C_0}{a_0}+s(I)\\right).$$",

      "conclusion": {
        "anchor": "stmt-lem-stopped-descent",
        "exact_quote": "Start at $I$ with a prescribed tilt $b_I$. Whenever a step is made, choose the child tilts by Lemma <a href=\"#lem:descent\" data-reference-type=\"ref\" data-reference=\"lem:descent\">11</a>, and suppose that its applicability condition holds at every square from which the procedure continues. Stop at a time $T$, no later than the terminal level. Write $m_i=m(Q_i,b_i)$ for the visited values, including the last value $m_T$. There is a constant $C_{\\mathrm{st}}$, independent of the depth and of the stopping rule, such that $$\\label{eq:stopped-descent}\n \\mathbb E\\left[\n      \\sum_{i=0}^{T}m_i\n      +\\sum_{i=0}^{T-1}\\abs{b_{i+1}-b_i}\n    \\right]\n \\le C_{\\mathrm{st}}\\bigl(m(I,b_I)+1\\bigr).$$ The second sum includes the step producing the last square, even if that square causes the procedure to stop.",
        "source_id": "PM-350.1"
      },
      "evidence": [
        {
          "anchor": "stmt-lem-stopped-descent",

          "source_id": "PM-350.1"
        }

      "conclusion": {
        "anchor": "stmt-lem-large-slope",
        "exact_quote": "For the fixed $L$ in Lemma <a href=\"#lem:descent\" data-reference-type=\"ref\" data-reference=\"lem:descent\">11</a>, there are constants $B_*\\ge1$ and $\\eta_*>0$ such that every admissible square with $s(Q)\\le1$ and $K_Q\\ge a_0$ satisfies $$\\label{eq:large-slope}\n \\abs{b}\\ge B_*,\n \\qquad m(Q,b)\\le\\eta_*\\abs{b}\n \\quad\\Longrightarrow\\quad N(Q)>L.$$",

      "exact_statement": "For the fixed $L$ in Lemma <a href=\"#lem:descent\" data-reference-type=\"ref\" data-reference=\"lem:descent\">11</a>, there are constants $B_*\\ge1$ and $\\eta_*>0$ such that every admissible square with $s(Q)\\le1$ and $K_Q\\ge a_0$ satisfies $$\\label{eq:large-slope}\n \\abs{b}\\ge B_*,\n \\qquad m(Q,b)\\le\\eta_*\\abs{b}\n \\quad\\Longrightarrow\\quad N(Q)>L.$$",

        "anchor": "stmt-prop-persistence",

    {
      "conclusion": {
        "anchor": "stmt-lem-packing",
        "exact_quote": "Let $\\mathcal F$ be a finite family of squares in the $A$-adic subdivision tree of a square $Q_0$, where $A\\ge2$. Suppose there are fixed $D\\ge1$, $\\delta>0$, and an integer $J\\ge0$ such that every $Q\\in\\mathcal F$ admits a measurable set $Y_Q\\subset DQ$ satisfying $$|Y_Q|\\ge\\delta|Q|,\n \\qquad\n \\#\\{F\\in\\mathcal F:s(F)<s(Q),\\ y\\in F\\}\\le J\n \\quad\\text{for almost every }y\\in Y_Q.$$ Then $$\\label{eq:packing}\n \\sum_{Q\\in\\mathcal F}|Q|\n \\le C(A,D,\\delta,J)|Q_0|.$$",
        "source_id": "PM-350.1"
      },
      "evidence": [
```

<a id="pm-s2"></a>
## 2. Definitions, validity and bounds

- **nodal set** ([source](#sec:introduction)): For a Riemannian metric $g$, let $\Delta_g=\operatorname{div}_g\nabla_g$, and write $Z_u=\{x:u(x)=0\}$ for the nodal set of a real function $u$.
- **Riemannian Hausdorff measure** ([source](#stmt-thm-main)): Here $\mathcal H_g^1$ is Hausdorff measure for the Riemannian distance.
- **frequency** ([source](#pm-h-3)): Set $k=\sqrt\lambda$, the frequency.
- **growth, normalized growth and untilted growth** ([source](#stmt-def-growth)): Fix an isothermal coordinate region in which $\Delta u+k^2p u=0$, and work in a relatively compact subset on which $p$ and $\nabla p$ have fixed uniform bounds. Set $R=1000$. Choose a root square $Q_0$, with side $s_0\le1$ and center $x_{Q_0}$, such that $\overline{\mathbb D_{2Rs_0}(x_{Q_0})}$ lies in this subset. For an integer $A\ge2$, subdivide each square into $A^2$ congruent squares and write $q\prec Q$ when $q$ is an immediate child of $Q$. For a square $Q$ of side $s=s(Q)$ and center $x_Q$, put $K_Q=ks$. For a real vector $b\in\mathbb R^2$, define $$\begin{aligned} E(Q,b) &=\log \frac{\norm{e^{-kb\cdot(x-x_Q)}u}_{L^2(\mathbb D_{Rs}(x_Q))}} {\norm{e^{-kb\cdot(x-x_Q)}u}_{L^2(\mathbb D_s(x_Q))}},\\ m(Q,b)&=\frac{E(Q,b)}{K_Q}, \qquad N(Q)=m(Q,0). \end{aligned} \label{eq:growth}$$ All planar norms here and below are unnormalized Euclidean norms. An average over children assigns weight $A^{-2}$ to each child.
- **disk ratio** ([source](#stmt-def-growth)): Set $R=1000$.
- **frequency-side product** ([source](#stmt-def-growth)): For a square $Q$ of side $s=s(Q)$ and center $x_Q$, put $K_Q=ks$.
- **child relation** ([source](#stmt-def-growth)): For an integer $A\ge2$, subdivide each square into $A^2$ congruent squares and write $q\prec Q$ when $q$ is an immediate child of $Q$.
- **average over children** ([source](#stmt-def-growth)): An average over children assigns weight $A^{-2}$ to each child.
- **excursion threshold** ([source](#stmt-def-excursions)): Choose a threshold $H>\max\{L,C_{\mathrm{init}}\}$.
- **excursion** ([source](#stmt-def-excursions)): Along each descending grid path, an excursion starts the first time $N(Q)>H$ and ends the first subsequent time $N(Q)\le L$.
- **entry** ([source](#stmt-def-excursions)): A square at which an excursion starts is called an *entry*.
- **active excursion** ([source](#stmt-def-excursions)): An excursion is *active* at a square if it has started and has not yet ended there.

| id | quantity | relation | value | regime |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| q-main-nodal-bound | Riemannian nodal length of an eigenfunction | ≤ | $C\sqrt\lambda$ | Constant depends on the surface and metric only; positive eigenvalue |
| q-disk-ratio | disk ratio R | = | $1000$ | Fixed once and for all |
| q-potential-l1-bound | L1 norm of the profile on the disk of radius eight | ≤ | $100\pi$ | Independent of the function |
| q-subdivision-contraction | average normalized growth of the children | ≤ | $\frac12\frac SK$ | Constants independent of the eigenfunction, scale and depth |
| q-stability-area | area of paths keeping high untilted growth | ≥ | $\abs{I}/2$ | Thresholds fixed by the descent constants |
| q-persistence-dilation | dilation factor of the witness region | = | $20A$ | A is the subdivision integer |
| q-terminal-mean-growth | average untilted growth over terminal squares | ≤ | $C$ | Independent of the eigenfunction and the number of levels |
| q-roy-fortin-length | nodal length in the small disk | ≤ | $C\max\{1,\beta\}$ | Universal constants |
| q-wavelength-length | nodal length in an admissible wavelength-scale square | ≤ | $Cs\bigl(1+E(I,0)\bigr)$ | Constants independent of the eigenfunction, frequency and side |

<a id="pm-s3"></a>
## 3. Proof roadmap

- **step-profile-extraction** [claim](#stmt-lem-profile-extraction) / [proof](#proof-stmt-lem-profile-extraction): Diagonal extraction over a countable basis of open sets, then definition of the profile as an infimum of limiting exponents. -> upper semicontinuous logarithmic profile with compact, open and weighted bounds
- **step-carleman** [claim](#stmt-lem-carleman) / [proof](#proof-stmt-lem-carleman): Contradiction with a sequence of approximate counterexamples, symmetric and skew-symmetric splitting of the conjugated operator and a commutator identity whose perpendicular energy gives the Hessian trace. -> local Carleman estimate
- **step-exceptional-gradient** [claim](#stmt-lem-exceptional-gradient) / [proof](#proof-stmt-lem-exceptional-gradient): Perturbation of a zero-gradient upper test by a linear term, then harmonic comparison. -> subharmonicity of the tested function
- **step-subharmonic-profile** [claim](#stmt-thm-subharmonic-profile) / [proof](#proof-stmt-lem-exceptional-gradient-2): Apply the Carleman estimate with a cutoff on an annulus and compare weighted exponents, then remove the affine part and use gradient removal. -> subharmonic logarithmic profile
- **step-initial-growth** [claim](#stmt-prop-initial-growth) / [proof](#proof-stmt-prop-initial-growth): Contradiction: extract a profile on the whole surface, show it is subharmonic with maximum zero, apply the strong maximum principle and contradict the open lower bound on the disk. -> nonvanishing and linear growth bound on a fixed disk
- **step-potential-decomposition** [claim](#stmt-lem-potential-decomposition) / [proof](#proof-stmt-lem-potential-decomposition): Submean inequality for an L1 bound, Riesz measure bound by a cutoff, then identification of the harmonic remainder. -> harmonic plus logarithmic potential decomposition with uniform bounds
- **step-affine-profile** [claim](#stmt-lem-affine-profile) / [proof](#proof-stmt-lem-affine-profile): Split sources into near and far, bound each by Taylor expansion or an explicit disk-average formula, then average over grid centres with dyadic annuli. -> affine correction vector with small average excess and bounded average slope
- **step-subdivision** [claim](#stmt-prop-subdivision) / [proof](#proof-stmt-prop-subdivision): Choose the subdivision integer and the slope constant first, then argue by contradiction with a rescaled sequence whose limiting profile is subharmonic and apply the affine correction. -> children tilts halving average normalized growth
- **step-one-step-descent** [claim](#stmt-lem-descent) / [proof](#proof-stmt-lem-descent): Choose the auxiliary scale so that the subdivision hypotheses follow from large growth or large tilt, then sum a geometric series along a path. -> conditional expectation inequalities with summable floors
- **step-stopped-descent** [claim](#stmt-lem-stopped-descent) / [proof](#proof-stmt-lem-stopped-descent): Finite conditional expectation identities on the finite path space, absorbing half of the growth sum. -> bound on expected total growth and tilt variation
- **step-large-slope** [claim](#stmt-lem-large-slope) / [proof](#proof-stmt-lem-large-slope): Contradiction with a sequence of rescaled squares, subharmonic limit equal to zero by the strong maximum principle, then undoing the tilt to force large untilted growth. -> large slope with small residual forces growth above the threshold
- **step-stability** [claim](#stmt-prop-stability) / [proof](#proof-stmt-prop-stability): Stop descent at the first failure of a tilt or residual inequality and bound the failure probability with Markov's inequality. -> half of the paths keep high untilted growth
- **step-entry-bound** [claim](#stmt-lem-entry-bound) / [proof](#proof-stmt-lem-entry-bound): Apply the subdivision step at the parent with zero tilt and compare tilted with untilted growth. -> upper bound on growth at an entry
- **step-persistence** [claim](#stmt-prop-persistence) / [proof](#proof-stmt-prop-persistence): Contradiction with rescaled entries, extraction of a nonconstant subharmonic profile, a Lebesgue point of its gradient with nonzero slope, then transfer to the stability proposition. -> witness set near each entry with bounded finer-entry count
- **step-packing** [claim](#stmt-lem-packing) / [proof](#proof-stmt-lem-packing): Rank squares by containment, select a separated subfamily, bound ancestry mismatches in boundary collars, and use Markov's inequality to bound overlaps. -> total area bound for the family
- **step-mean-growth** [claim](#stmt-thm-mean-growth) / [proof](#proof-stmt-thm-mean-growth): Split terminal paths into inactive and those with a final active entry, run stopped descent from each entry, then sum entry contributions using the packing bound. -> bounded average untilted growth over terminal squares
- **step-roy-fortin** [claim](#stmt-thm-local-length) / [proof](#stmt-thm-local-length): Cited planar nodal length theorem used as a black box. -> local nodal length bound by logarithmic doubling
- **step-wavelength-length** [claim](#stmt-lem-wavelength-length) / [proof](#proof-stmt-lem-wavelength-length): Rescale a square to the planar normalization, use an interior regularity estimate and Sobolev embedding to pass from supremum to L2 norms, and apply the cited planar theorem. -> nodal length in a wavelength-scale square
- **step-main-theorem** [claim](#stmt-thm-main) / [proof](#proof-stmt-lem-wavelength-length-2): Cover the surface by finitely many coordinate root squares, subdivide to the wavelength scale, sum the local length bounds using bounded mean growth, and transfer to Riemannian Hausdorff measure. -> nodal length at most a constant times the square root of the eigenvalue

<a id="pm-s4"></a>
## 4. Engineering Triptych

### Panel 1 - epistemic contract

- **inv-nodal-length-bound**: On one fixed closed connected smooth surface, a single constant, depending only on the surface and its metric, bounds the nodal length of every nonzero real eigenfunction by a multiple of the square root of its positive eigenvalue.
- **inv-initial-growth-linear**: On a fixed coordinate disk, every nonzero eigenfunction has a nonzero local norm, and the logarithmic ratio of global to local norm is at most a fixed multiple of the frequency.
- **inv-planar-length-from-growth**: For a nonzero real solution of a small-potential planar Helmholtz-type equation, the nodal length in a small disk is controlled by the logarithmic ratio of sup norms on two concentric disks.

### Panel 2 - negative knowledge

- **anti-higher-dimension**: Apply the square-root nodal bound to nodal hypersurfaces of manifolds of dimension three or more. -> The proof relies on a two-dimensional Carleman commutator feature and isothermal coordinates, so nothing is established in higher dimension.
- **anti-boundary-or-open**: Apply the theorem to a drum or plate with a boundary. -> The statement is only for closed surfaces; boundary behaviour is not covered.
- **anti-uniform-constant-across-metrics**: Reuse one constant for different metrics or for a varying family of surfaces. -> The constant is only asserted for the fixed pair of surface and metric.

### Panel 3 - reference implementation and oracle

Applicability: `not_applicable`. The results are existence statements for constants, proved by contradiction and compactness arguments over sequences of eigenfunctions, so no finite procedure computes anything; the main theorem only asserts that some finite constant depending on the surface exists.

<a id="pm-s6"></a>
## 6. Applicability limits and search

- **limit-closed-smooth-surface** (hypothesis): The main bound is stated only for a closed connected smooth Riemannian surface. Surfaces with boundary, non-compact surfaces and non-smooth metrics are not covered. Blocked use: ['Applying the square-root nodal length bound to a drum or plate with Dirichlet or Neumann boundary', 'Applying the bound to a surface with a rough metric']
- **limit-constant-depends-on-metric** (formal_scope): The constant is finite and depends on the fixed surface and metric. No numerical value is given, and nothing is claimed about uniformity across different metrics. Blocked use: ['Reusing one constant for a family of varying metrics', 'Quoting a numeric value of the constant as a guaranteed budget']
- **limit-two-dimensional-argument** (hypothesis): The Carleman step relies on a feature specific to dimension two, where the commutator sees the full trace of the weight Hessian. The method gives nothing in higher dimension. Blocked use: ['Extending the nodal bound to nodal hypersurfaces in dimension three or more']
- **limit-nonconstructive-constants** (nonconstructive): The subdivision constants are obtained by contradiction with a sequence of rescaled eigenfunctions and a compactness argument, so they are not explicit. Blocked use: ['Computing explicit values of the subdivision constants from the proof', 'Presenting the subdivision step as an algorithm with a proven running time']
- **limit-subdivision-smallness** (hypothesis): One subdivision step needs the stated smallness conditions on the frequency-side product relative to the growth parameter and the tilt. Outside them the halving of the average normalized growth is not asserted. Blocked use: ['Applying the halving estimate when the frequency-side product is large compared with the growth parameter']
- **limit-wavelength-scale-length** (hypothesis): The local nodal length bound on a square needs the frequency-side product to be at most a small fixed number and the square to be admissible. - **limit-planar-theorem-small-potential** (hypothesis): The cited planar nodal length theorem requires a potential with small sup norm on the disk of radius three. Blocked use: ['Using the planar bound for an equation with a large potential without rescaling']

<a id="pm-formal"></a>
### Formal statement (Lean 4)

Corpus status: formalized in the source corpus (toolchain `leanprover/lean4:v4.34.1`, Mathlib `d13f23b723b8a846827a245b89c10fc7d3f11612`). Not re-checked here; the statements give the exact quantifiers and hypotheses, not a proof.

Scope note of family 350 (`lean/docs/350.md`, verbatim):

> The following describes the scope of the Lean formalization related to the following accompanying paper(s):
>
> - [Sharp nodal length on smooth surfaces](../../preprints/Sharp-nodal-length-on-smooth-surfaces-September-23-2026/paper.pdf)
> - [Smooth counterexamples to Yau's nodal upper bound in dimensions three and four](../../preprints/Smooth-counterexamples-to-Yaus-nodal-upper-bound-in-dimensions-three-and-four-September-23-2026/paper.pdf)
> - [Power-law violations of Yau's nodal upper bound](../../preprints/Power-law-violations-of-Yaus-nodal-upper-bound-September-23-2026/paper.pdf)
>
> ## Scope
>
> Yau's nodal-set conjecture predicts nodal size of order $\sqrt\lambda$ for Laplace eigenfunctions of eigenvalue $\lambda$. The formalization proves the upper bound on every fixed smooth closed connected Riemannian surface: there is a surface-dependent constant $C$ such that the one-dimensional Hausdorff measure of the zero set of every nonzero real eigenfunction with $\lambda>0$ is at most $C\sqrt\lambda$. The known lower bound is not part of this selected statement.
>
> The formalized results contradict Yau's proposed $O(\sqrt\lambda)$ upper bound for nodal measure in dimensions three and four. On $S^3$, every prescribed smooth neighborhood of the round metric contains one fixed smooth metric with an eigenfunction sequence of unbounded nodal-measure ratio. A second fixed metric on $S^2\times T^2$ has the same property. In both cases the eigenvalues tend to infinity and the intrinsic nodal measures are finite.
>
> Yau's nodal upper-bound conjecture predicts that the nodal measure of an eigenfunction is bounded by a constant times the square root of its eigenvalue. The formalized counterexample fixes one smooth metric on $S^4\times S^1$ and a sequence of nonzero smooth eigenfunctions whose positive eigenvalues tend to infinity, while the intrinsic four-dimensional nodal measures divided by the square roots of the eigenvalues tend to infinity. The metric is fixed throughout the sequence.

Comparator source `NodalLength.lean` (2104 bytes, sha256 `bb3e25d30031fb3469baf316180d05dafd6e4c50dab40353771f211f430d2f74`), verbatim:

```lean
import Mathlib

namespace OAI

noncomputable section

open scoped ContDiff Bundle ENNReal
open Bundle Manifold MeasureTheory

namespace SharpNodal

abbrev Plane := EuclideanSpace ℝ (Fin 2)

variable {M : Type*} [MetricSpace M] [ChartedSpace Plane M]
  [IsManifold 𝓘(ℝ, Plane) ∞ M]
  [RiemannianBundle (fun x : M => TangentSpace 𝓘(ℝ, Plane) x)]

def coordinateMetric (x : M) (y : Plane) : Matrix (Fin 2) (Fin 2) ℝ :=
  fun i j => inner ℝ
    (mfderiv 𝓘(ℝ, Plane) 𝓘(ℝ, Plane) (chartAt Plane x).symm y
      (EuclideanSpace.single i 1))
    (mfderiv 𝓘(ℝ, Plane) 𝓘(ℝ, Plane) (chartAt Plane x).symm y
      (EuclideanSpace.single j 1))

def coordinateDensity (x : M) (y : Plane) : ℝ :=
  Real.sqrt (coordinateMetric x y).det

def coordPartial (f : Plane → ℝ) (i : Fin 2) (y : Plane) : ℝ :=
  fderiv ℝ f y (EuclideanSpace.single i 1)

def laplaceBeltrami (u : M → ℝ) (x : M) : ℝ :=
  (coordinateDensity x (chartAt Plane x x))⁻¹ *
    ∑ i : Fin 2, coordPartial (fun y => coordinateDensity x y *
      ∑ j : Fin 2, ((coordinateMetric x y)⁻¹ i j) *
        coordPartial (u ∘ (chartAt Plane x).symm) j y) i (chartAt Plane x x)

def nodalSet (u : M → ℝ) : Set M := {x | u x = 0}

def nodalLength (u : M → ℝ) : ℝ≥0∞ :=
  letI : MeasurableSpace M := borel M
  letI : BorelSpace M := ⟨rfl⟩
  Measure.hausdorffMeasure 1 (nodalSet u)

def MainStatement (M : Type*) [MetricSpace M] [ChartedSpace Plane M]
    [IsManifold 𝓘(ℝ, Plane) ∞ M]
    [RiemannianBundle (fun x : M => TangentSpace 𝓘(ℝ, Plane) x)] : Prop :=
  ∃ C : ℝ, 0 ≤ C ∧ ∀ (lam : ℝ), 0 < lam → ∀ (u : M → ℝ),
    ContMDiff 𝓘(ℝ, Plane) 𝓘(ℝ) ∞ u → u ≠ 0 →
    (∀ x, -laplaceBeltrami u x = lam * u x) →
    nodalLength u ≤ ENNReal.ofReal (C * Real.sqrt lam)

theorem Main [CompactSpace M] [ConnectedSpace M]
    [IsContMDiffRiemannianBundle 𝓘(ℝ,Plane) ∞ Plane
      (fun x:M=>TangentSpace 𝓘(ℝ,Plane) x)]
    [IsRiemannianManifold 𝓘(ℝ,Plane) M] : MainStatement M := by
  sorry

end SharpNodal

end

end OAI
```

A second fixed metric on $S^2\\times T^2$ has the same property. In both cases the eigenvalues tend to infinity and the intrinsic nodal measures are finite.\n\nYau's nodal upper-bound conjecture predicts that the nodal measure of an eigenfunction is bounded by a constant times the square root of its eigenvalue. The formalized counterexample fixes one smooth metric on $S^4\\times S^1$ and a sequence of nonzero smooth eigenfunctions whose positive eigenvalues tend to infinity, while the intrinsic four-dimensional nodal measures divided by the square roots of the eigenvalues tend to infinity. The metric is fixed throughout the sequence."
```json
  },
  "status": "present",
  "toolchain": "leanprover/lean4:v4.34.1"
}
```
<!-- PM:END pm.formal.v2 -->

<a id="pm-s7"></a>
## 7. Complete mathematical paper


We prove that a nonzero real Laplace eigenfunction with eigenvalue <i>λ</i> &gt; 0 on a fixed smooth closed connected Riemannian surface has nodal length at most $`C\sqrt\lambda`$. Together with the known lower bound, this proves Yau's conjecture in this setting.

<a id="pm-h-1"></a>
#### Statements index

<a id="sec:introduction"></a>
### Introduction and main result

For a Riemannian metric $g$, let $\Delta_g=\operatorname{div}_g\nabla_g$, and write $Z_u=\{x:u(x)=0\}$ for the nodal set of a real function $u$. We prove the following bound for one arbitrary fixed smooth surface.

<a id="stmt-thm-main"></a>
<div id="thm:main" class="theorem">

**Theorem 1**. *Let $(M,g)$ be a closed connected $C^\infty$ Riemannian surface. There is a finite constant $C=C(M,g)$ such that every nonzero real Laplace eigenfunction satisfying $$-\Delta_g u=\lambda u,\qquad \lambda>0,$$ obeys $$\mathcal H_g^1(Z_u)\le C\sqrt\lambda.$$ Here $\mathcal H_g^1$ is Hausdorff measure for the Riemannian distance. The same constant applies to every eigenfunction in every positive eigenspace.*

</div>

Yau’s conjecture asks for upper and lower bounds of order $\sqrt\lambda$ for the $(n-1)$-dimensional measure of the nodal set on a smooth closed $n$-dimensional manifold <a id="pm-cite-1"></a>[[Yau1982](#ref-Yau1982)]. Donnelly and Fefferman proved both bounds for real analytic manifolds and metrics <a id="pm-cite-2"></a>[[DonnellyFefferman1988](#ref-DonnellyFefferman1988), Theorem 1.2]. For smooth surfaces, Brüning established the lower bound <a id="pm-cite-3"></a>[[Bruning1978](#ref-Bruning1978), Satz 2]; Logunov subsequently proved the lower bound in every dimension <a id="pm-cite-4"></a>[[Logunov2018Lower](#ref-Logunov2018Lower), Theorem 1.1]. For the surface upper bound with a smooth metric, Donnelly and Fefferman proved $C\lambda^{3/4}$ <a id="pm-cite-5"></a>[[DonnellyFefferman1990](#ref-DonnellyFefferman1990)], and Dong gave a different proof at the same order <a id="pm-cite-6"></a>[[Dong1992](#ref-Dong1992)]. Logunov and Malinnikova then obtained $C\lambda^{3/4-\beta}$ for a universal $\beta\in(0,1/4)$ <a id="pm-cite-7"></a>[[LogunovMalinnikova2018](#ref-LogunovMalinnikova2018), Equation (1.1)]. Together with the known lower bound, Theorem <a href="#thm:main" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:main">1</a> resolves Yau’s conjecture positively for smooth closed surfaces.

<a id="pm-h-3"></a>
#### Mean growth as the target

Set $k=\sqrt\lambda$, the frequency. In local smooth isothermal coordinates the metric is $g=p(x)(dx_1^2+dx_2^2)$, with $p>0$ smooth, as in <a id="pm-cite-8"></a>[[DeTurckKazdan1981](#ref-DeTurckKazdan1981), Section 1], and the eigenfunction equation becomes $$\Delta u+k^2p(x)u=0.$$ Nazarov, Polterovich, and Sodin advocated a statistical approach to local doubling and sketched its relation to nodal length, building on Nadirashvili’s approach <a id="pm-cite-9"></a>[[NazarovPolterovichSodin2005](#ref-NazarovPolterovichSodin2005), Section 7.3]. Roy-Fortin established precise comparisons between growth and nodal length <a id="pm-cite-10"></a>[[RoyFortin2015](#ref-RoyFortin2015), [RoyFortinLq](#ref-RoyFortinLq)]. We prove the uniform mean growth bound needed for the sharp upper estimate and use his planar theorem for the final transfer.

Here is why an average suffices. For a coordinate square $Q$ of side $s$ and center $x_Q$, consider the unweighted logarithmic growth $$E(Q,0)=\log
 \frac{\|u\|_{L^2(\mathbb D_{Rs}(x_Q))}}
      {\|u\|_{L^2(\mathbb D_s(x_Q))}},$$ where $R$ is the fixed large disk ratio chosen below and $\mathbb D_r(x)$ is the Euclidean disk of radius $r$. The norms are unnormalized. Subdivide a fixed coordinate square into congruent squares of side $s$ comparable to $k^{-1}$, stopping once $ks$ is sufficiently small; call these the terminal squares. The estimate proved below gives $$\mathop{\mathrm{avg}}_{Q\text{ terminal}} E(Q,0)\le C.$$ On each such square, Roy-Fortin’s theorem, after rescaling and a change from supremum norms to $L^2$ norms, gives $$\mathcal H^1(Q\cap Z_u)\le Cs\bigl(1+E(Q,0)\bigr).$$ There are $O(s^{-2})$ squares in a fixed coordinate region. Summing the local estimates therefore gives $O(s^{-1})=O(k)$ nodal length. The precise grids, radii and constants are specified in Sections <a href="#sec:subdivision" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:subdivision">3</a> and <a href="#sec:length" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:length">6</a>. The task is thus to control the average growth while allowing large values on individual squares.

<a id="pm-h-4"></a>
#### Affine correction and persistent growth

The analytic step extracts logarithmic profiles from norms measured on an exponential scale. With the required separation from the rescaled frequency $ks$, a localized Carleman estimate makes these profiles subharmonic, even after arbitrarily large affine tilts. Its decisive feature is two-dimensional: when the normalized derivative energy is perpendicular to the weight gradient, the commutator sees the full trace of the weight Hessian.

A local affine part of a profile presents an obstruction to decay: its oscillation and $ks$ decrease by the same factor under subdivision. We subtract that affine part and carry its slope separately. The remaining profile growth decreases faster than the side length on average, while the changes of slope have bounded average cost. Section <a href="#sec:subdivision" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:subdivision">3</a> quantifies both estimates; Section <a href="#sec:descent" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:descent">4</a> sums their costs along paths that may stop.

To use this improvement through many scales, we divide a grid path into excursions of high growth. A newly entered excursion produces a nearby set of positive area on which high growth persists to the terminal scale. These witness sets may lie outside their entry squares. A packing argument controls them by counting discrepancies between spatial membership and grid ancestry; those discrepancies are confined to summable boundary collars. This geometric argument is stated separately in Lemma <a href="#lem:packing" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:packing">18</a>. It permits the analytic persistence statement to be used without requiring its witnesses to lie inside the original squares.

Section <a href="#sec:profiles" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:profiles">2</a> constructs the subharmonic limits and establishes the initial growth bound. Section <a href="#sec:subdivision" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:subdivision">3</a> proves the affine subdivision estimate, and Section <a href="#sec:descent" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:descent">4</a> controls stopped descents and persistent slopes. Section <a href="#sec:persistence" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:persistence">5</a> proves persistence at entry, packs the excursions, and bounds the terminal mean growth. Section <a href="#sec:length" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:length">6</a> completes the nodal-length estimate. All coordinate constructions are local, so no orientability assumption is needed.

<a id="sec:profiles"></a>
### Subharmonic limits of tilted norms

We record exponential growth through logarithms of squared local $L^2$ norms. Pointwise logarithms are singular at zeros; the compactness statement below instead controls masses on all compact and open sets, which is what later comparisons of norms require. Its compatibility with affine weights will allow us to change the tilt as we subdivide. Throughout the planar arguments, norms and integrals are Euclidean and are not normalized by area. We write $\mathbb D_r(a)$ for the open Euclidean disk of radius $r$ centered at $a$, and $\mathbb D_r=\mathbb D_r(0)$; a bar denotes closure. For a measurable planar set $E$, the notation $|E|$ denotes its Euclidean area.

<a id="stmt-lem-profile-extraction"></a>
<div id="lem:profile-extraction" class="lemma">

<a id="eq:profile-bounds"></a>
**Lemma 2** (Extraction of a logarithmic profile). *Let $X$ be an open subset of $\mathbb R^2$ or a compact smooth surface. Suppose that $S_j\to\infty$ and that $\mu_j$ are nonnegative Borel measures on $X$ with $\mu_j(X)\le 1$. There are a subsequence and an upper semicontinuous function $V\colon X\to[-\infty,0]$ such that, for every compact $F\subset X$ and every open $G\subset X$, $$\label{eq:profile-bounds}
 \limsup_{j\to\infty}\frac{\log\mu_j(F)}{2S_j}\le\sup_F V,
 \qquad
 \liminf_{j\to\infty}\frac{\log\mu_j(G)}{2S_j}\ge\sup_G V.$$ Here $\log 0=\sup\varnothing=-\infty$. More generally, if continuous real functions $f_j$ converge locally uniformly to $f$, then $$\begin{aligned}
 \limsup_{j\to\infty}\frac{1}{2S_j}
       \log\int_F e^{2S_j f_j}\,d\mu_j&\le\sup_F(V+f),\\
 \liminf_{j\to\infty}\frac{1}{2S_j}
       \log\int_G e^{2S_j f_j}\,d\mu_j&\ge\sup_G(V+f).
 \end{aligned}$$ The same subsequence works for all these sets and weights.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-profile-extraction"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-profile-extraction</summary>

*Proof.* Choose a countable basis $\{G_i\}_{i\ge1}$ consisting of relatively compact open sets. A diagonal subsequence makes all the limits $$\ell_i=\lim_{j\to\infty}\frac{\log\mu_j(G_i)}{2S_j}
        \in[-\infty,0]$$ exist. Define $V(x)=\inf_{i:x\in G_i}\ell_i$. If $V(x)<a$, one of these basis neighborhoods has $\ell_i<a$, and $V<a$ throughout that neighborhood. Thus $V$ is upper semicontinuous.

For any finite $a>\sup_F V$, every point of $F$ has a basis neighborhood with exponent less than $a$. A finite subcover and $\mu_j(F)\le\sum_i\mu_j(G_i)$ give the compact upper bound by $a$. Letting $a\downarrow\sup_F V$ proves the first inequality, also when this supremum is $-\infty$. If $x\in G$, a basis neighborhood $G_i$ with $x\in G_i\subset G$ gives a lower bound by $\ell_i\ge V(x)$, proving the second inequality.

For the weighted upper bound, take $a>\sup_F(V+f)$ and choose the neighborhood at each point small enough that its limiting measure exponent plus the supremum of $f$ on it is less than $a$. This is possible by continuity of $f$ and the definition of $V$, including at points where $V=-\infty$. A finite cover and uniform convergence of $f_j$ on its compact closure give the claimed upper bound. For the lower bound at a point $x\in G$ with $V(x)>-\infty$, choose $G_i$ compactly contained in $G$ so small that $f\ge f(x)-\varepsilon$ there. The weighted integral over $G_i$ has lower exponent at least $\ell_i+f(x)-\varepsilon$. Let $\varepsilon\downarrow0$ and then take the supremum over $x$. ◻

</details>

We call $V$ a *logarithmic profile*. Multiplying the measures by positive densities uniformly bounded above and below on compact sets does not change the profile bounds: the logarithms of these bounds disappear after division by $2S_j$. We may also use the compact upper bound for a relatively compact set by taking its closure. Normalizing mass on an open disk alone does not assert that the profile has a finite value inside the disk; the identically $-\infty$ case remains possible.

<a id="stmt-thm-subharmonic-profile"></a>
<div id="thm:subharmonic-profile" class="theorem">

<a id="eq:profile-hypotheses"></a>
**Theorem 3** (Subharmonicity of logarithmic profiles). *On a fixed planar disk $\Omega$, let real smooth functions $U_j,p_j$ satisfy $$\Delta U_j+K_j^2p_jU_j=0,$$ where $K_j\ge0$. Let $S_j>0$, $B_j\in\mathbb R^2$, and $D_j=S_j+|B_j|$. Assume $$\label{eq:profile-hypotheses}
 \begin{gathered}
 S_j\longrightarrow\infty,\qquad
 \frac{K_j}{D_j}\longrightarrow0,\\
 \sup_j\|p_j\|_\infty<\infty,\qquad
 \frac{K_j^2\|\nabla p_j\|_\infty}{S_jD_j}\longrightarrow0.
 \end{gathered}$$ Local versions of the coefficient assumptions on relatively compact subdisks suffice. For positive constants $c_j$, suppose the measures $$d\mu_j=c_j e^{-2B_j\cdot x}|U_j(x)|^2\,dx$$ have mass at most one on $\Omega$. Every upper semicontinuous logarithmic profile with the compact, open, and weighted bounds of Lemma <a href="#lem:profile-extraction" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:profile-extraction">2</a> is subharmonic on $\Omega$, with the identically $-\infty$ function allowed.*

</div>

The main estimate concerns weights whose normalized gradient is nonzero. It is essential that the next lemma allows $D_j/S_j$ to be arbitrarily large.

<a id="stmt-lem-carleman"></a>
<div id="lem:carleman" class="lemma">

<a id="eq:carleman"></a>
**Lemma 4** (A local Carleman estimate). *Assume the coefficient and parameter hypotheses of Theorem <a href="#thm:subharmonic-profile" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:subharmonic-profile">3</a>. After passing to a subsequence, write $$S_j/D_j\to d,\qquad B_j/D_j\to b,
 \qquad d\ge0,\quad d+|b|=1.$$ Let $\chi$ be a real smooth function near $x_0\in\Omega$ such that $\Delta\chi(x_0)<0$ and $b+d\nabla\chi(x_0)\ne0$. Put $T_j(x)=-B_j\cdot x-S_j\chi(x)$. There are a neighborhood $\Omega_0$ of $x_0$, a constant $c>0$, and an index $j_0$ such that $$\label{eq:carleman}
 \|e^{T_j}(\Delta+K_j^2p_j)w\|_2
 \ge c\sqrt{S_j}\,D_j\,\|e^{T_j}w\|_2$$ for all $j\ge j_0$ and all real $w\in C_c^\infty(\Omega_0)$.*

</div>

The two parameters reflect different derivatives of the weight: $\nabla T_j=-B_j-S_j\nabla\chi$ has scale $D_j$, whereas $T_j''=-S_j\chi''$ has scale $S_j$. The affine tilt can therefore enlarge the gradient without enlarging the curvature.

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-carleman"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-carleman</summary>

<a id="eq:commutator"></a>
*Proof.* Suppress the index and write $v=e^T w$. The conjugated operator is $P_++P_-$, where $$P_+=\Delta+|\nabla T|^2+K^2p,
 \qquad P_-=-2\nabla T\cdot\nabla-\Delta T.$$ On compactly supported functions, $P_+$ is symmetric and $P_-$ is skew-symmetric. Integration by parts gives $$\label{eq:commutator}
 \begin{split}
 2\langle P_+v,P_-v\rangle
  ={}&4\int T''(\nabla v,\nabla v)\\
    &+\int\bigl(4T''(\nabla T,\nabla T)-\Delta^2T
             +2K^2\nabla p\cdot\nabla T\bigr)v^2.
 \end{split}$$ For clarity, the contribution from $\Delta$ is $4\int T''(\nabla v,\nabla v)-\int\Delta^2T\,v^2$. Multiplication by a real function $a$ contributes $2\int\nabla a\cdot\nabla T\,v^2$; applying this with $a=|\nabla T|^2+K^2p$ gives the remaining terms in Equation <a href="#eq:commutator" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:commutator">[eq:commutator]</a>.

If Equation <a href="#eq:carleman" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:carleman">[eq:carleman]</a> failed on every sufficiently small neighborhood, a diagonal choice would give a subsequence and real smooth $v_j$ with supports shrinking to $x_0$, such that $$\|v_j\|_2=1,
 \qquad
 \|(P_++P_-)v_j\|_2=o(\sqrt{S_j}D_j).$$ All constants below are uniform on one fixed small neighborhood of $x_0$. There, $|\nabla T|\le CD$, $|T''|+|\Delta^2T|\le CS$, and the last hypothesis in Equation <a href="#eq:profile-hypotheses" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:profile-hypotheses">[eq:profile-hypotheses]</a> controls the potential term in Equation <a href="#eq:commutator" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:commutator">[eq:commutator]</a>. Set $X=\|P_+v\|_2$, $Y=\|P_-v\|_2$, and $G=\|\nabla v\|_2$. Expanding the squared norm and using the identity gives $$X^2+Y^2\le CS(G^2+D^2).$$ Testing $P_+$ against $v$ gives $G^2\le CD^2+X$, since $K=o(D)$. Combining the two inequalities first yields $X\le C(S+\sqrt S D)$ and then $$G\le CD,
 \qquad X+Y\le C\sqrt S D.$$ Here $D\ge S\to\infty$ absorbs the term $S$.

Uniformly on the shrinking supports, $$\nabla T_j/D_j\to a=-b-d\nabla\chi(x_0)\ne0,
 \qquad T_j''/S_j\to H=-\chi''(x_0).$$ The preceding bounds give $$\frac{\sqrt{S_j}}{D_j}\le\frac1{\sqrt{S_j}}\longrightarrow0.$$ Dividing the identity obtained by testing $P_+$ against $v_j$ by $D_j^2$ therefore shows $$\int|\nabla v_j/D_j|^2\longrightarrow |a|^2.$$ Likewise, $$\left\|\frac{\nabla T_j}{D_j}\cdot
                \frac{\nabla v_j}{D_j}\right\|_2
 \le \frac{\|P_-v_j\|_2+\|\Delta T_j\,v_j\|_2}{2D_j^2}
 \longrightarrow0.$$ Replacing $\nabla T_j/D_j$ by its uniform limit consequently gives $\|a\cdot\nabla v_j/D_j\|_2\to0$.

Let $e$ be a unit vector perpendicular to $a$. In dimension two, the energy perpendicular to $a$ has only this one direction. Decomposing $\nabla v_j/D_j$ in the orthonormal basis $\{a/|a|,e\}$, the parallel component tends to zero in $L^2$, whereas the squared norm of the perpendicular component tends to $|a|^2$. Hence the first term in Equation <a href="#eq:commutator" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:commutator">[eq:commutator]</a>, divided by $S_jD_j^2$, tends to $4|a|^2H(e,e)$. The other main Hessian term tends to $4H(a,a)$. The two remaining terms tend to zero: their absolute values after this division are bounded by $$\frac{C}{D_j^2}
 \quad\hbox{and}\quad
 \frac{CK_j^2\|\nabla p_j\|_\infty}{S_jD_j},$$ respectively. We have thus proved $$\begin{aligned}
 \frac{2\langle P_+v_j,P_-v_j\rangle}{S_jD_j^2}
 &\longrightarrow 4\bigl(|a|^2H(e,e)+H(a,a)\bigr)\\
 &=4|a|^2\operatorname{tr}H>0.
 \end{aligned}$$ The squared norms of $P_+v_j$ and $P_-v_j$ are nonnegative, so this contradicts the assumed smallness of $\|(P_++P_-)v_j\|_2^2$. ◻

</details>

We also need to remove one possible exceptional gradient from the upper-test criterion. The following elementary perturbation does so without any initial integrability assumption. An upper test at a finite point $x_0$ is a smooth function agreeing with the tested function at $x_0$ and dominating it in a neighborhood of $x_0$. This is an upper-semicontinuous subharmonic variant of the standard viscosity exceptional-gradient-removal principle; compare <a id="pm-cite-11"></a>[[ImbertSilvestre2013](#ref-ImbertSilvestre2013), Section 5, Lemma 6]. We give the local proof for functions allowed to take the value $-\infty$, rather than apply that lemma directly.

<a id="stmt-lem-exceptional-gradient"></a>
<div id="lem:exceptional-gradient" class="lemma">

**Lemma 5** (Removal of the exceptional gradient). *Let $W\colon\Omega\to[-\infty,\infty)$ be upper semicontinuous and locally bounded above on a connected planar domain. Suppose every smooth function $\psi$ touching $W$ from above at a finite point $x_0$, with $\nabla\psi(x_0)\ne0$, satisfies $\Delta\psi(x_0)\ge0$. Then $W$ is subharmonic, with the identically $-\infty$ function allowed.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-exceptional-gradient"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-exceptional-gradient</summary>

*Proof.* Suppose first that an upper test at $0$ has zero gradient and negative Laplacian; translate the point to $0$ if necessary. A quadratic Taylor polynomial with its Hessian increased by a sufficiently small positive multiple of the identity gives a quadratic upper test $h$ with $$\begin{gathered}
 W(0)=h(0),\qquad \nabla h(0)=0,\qquad
 \operatorname{tr}h''<0,\\
 W(x)<h(x)\quad(0<|x|\le r)
 \end{gathered}$$ for some small $r>0$. Choose a unit eigenvector $e$ of $h''$ with eigenvalue $\alpha<0$. For small $t>0$, maximize $W(x)-h(x)+t e\cdot x$ on $\overline{\mathbb D_r}$. Upper semicontinuity and the strict boundary gap ensure that a maximizer $x_t$ is interior. Its value is at least the value $0$ at the origin, and $W-h\le0$, so $e\cdot x_t\ge0$. At $x_t$ an upper test is $h(x)-t e\cdot x$, shifted by its contact constant. Its gradient has component $$e\cdot\nabla(h-t e\cdot x)(x_t)
 =\alpha\, e\cdot x_t-t<0,$$ while its Laplacian is still negative. This contradicts the hypothesis. All smooth upper tests at finite points therefore have nonnegative Laplacian.

We obtain harmonic comparison directly. Let $\overline{\mathbb D_r(a)}\subset\Omega$, let $h$ be harmonic in $\mathbb D_r(a)$ and continuous on its closure, and suppose $W\le h$ on the boundary. If $W-h$ were positive somewhere inside, then for sufficiently small $\varepsilon>0$ the function $$h_\varepsilon(x)=h(x)+\varepsilon(r^2-|x-a|^2)$$ would still lie below $W$ at some interior point. A positive maximum of $W-h_\varepsilon$ is attained at an interior finite point. Adding this maximum to $h_\varepsilon$ supplies an upper test with Laplacian $-4\varepsilon<0$, again a contradiction. Thus $W$ satisfies harmonic comparison. For upper semicontinuous functions this is the usual characterization of subharmonicity, including the identically $-\infty$ case; see <a id="pm-cite-12"></a>[[Ransford1995](#ref-Ransford1995), Chapter 2]. In particular, local integrability and the submean property of a nontrivial subharmonic function are consequences here, not assumptions used in the argument. ◻

</details>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-exceptional-gradient-2"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-exceptional-gradient</summary>

*Proof of Theorem <a href="#thm:subharmonic-profile" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:subharmonic-profile">3</a>.* Fix such a profile $V$ and pass to a further subsequence so that $S_j/D_j\to d$ and $B_j/D_j\to b$ as in Lemma <a href="#lem:carleman" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:carleman">4</a>. The profile bounds remain valid on this subsequence. Suppose a smooth $\chi$ touches $V$ from above at a finite point $x_0$, and suppose $$\Delta\chi(x_0)<0,
 \qquad b+d\nabla\chi(x_0)\ne0.$$ By adding a small positive multiple of $|x-x_0|^4$, we make the touching strict on a sufficiently small closed disk without altering the gradient or Hessian at $x_0$. Take this disk inside the neighborhood in Lemma <a href="#lem:carleman" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:carleman">4</a>.

Choose fixed radii $0<r_0<r_1<r_2<r_3$ inside that neighborhood. Let $\zeta$ be a smooth cutoff equal to one on $\mathbb D_{r_1}(x_0)$ and supported in $\mathbb D_{r_2}(x_0)$. Its derivatives are supported in the annulus between these two disks. Set $$G=\mathbb D_{r_0/2}(x_0),\qquad
 F=\{x:r_0\le |x-x_0|\le r_3\}.$$ We may choose the radii so that the strict touching holds on the whole closed outer disk. Then $F$ is a compact annulus, $\sup_F(V-\chi)<0$, and $\sup_G(V-\chi)=0$.

For completeness, the cutoff error can be controlled with a prefactor independent of the ratio $D_j/S_j$. Choose a smooth $\theta$ supported in the interior of $F$ and equal to one near the derivatives of $\zeta$. Testing the equation for $U_j$ against $\theta^2e^{2T_j}U_j$ and integrating by parts bounds $$\begin{aligned}
 \int\theta^2e^{2T_j}|\nabla U_j|^2
 &\le C\int_F e^{2T_j}
       \bigl(|\nabla\theta|^2+
       \theta^2|\nabla T_j|^2+K_j^2|p_j|\theta^2\bigr)|U_j|^2\\
 &\le CD_j^2\int_F e^{2T_j}|U_j|^2.
 \end{aligned}$$ The first inequality absorbs half of the gradient integral by Cauchy–Schwarz; the second uses $K_j=o(D_j)$ and $D_j\to\infty$. Since $$(\Delta+K_j^2p_j)(\zeta U_j)
   =(\Delta\zeta)U_j+2\nabla\zeta\cdot\nabla U_j,$$ Lemma <a href="#lem:carleman" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:carleman">4</a> gives $$c\sqrt{S_j}D_j\|e^{T_j}U_j\|_{L^2(G)}
 \le CD_j\|e^{T_j}U_j\|_{L^2(F)}.$$ Cancel $D_j$ before comparing exponential rates. Squaring and multiplying by the common normalization constant $c_j$ yields $$S_j\int_G e^{-2S_j\chi}\,d\mu_j
       \le C\int_F e^{-2S_j\chi}\,d\mu_j.$$ The weighted bounds of Lemma <a href="#lem:profile-extraction" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:profile-extraction">2</a>, together with $\log S_j/S_j\to0$, now give the contradiction $$0=\sup_G(V-\chi)\le\sup_F(V-\chi)<0.$$ In particular, no bound on $\log D_j/S_j$ was required.

We have established the upper-test inequality whenever $b+d\nabla\chi(x_0)\ne0$. If $d=0$, then $|b|=1$ and there is no exceptional gradient; harmonic comparison follows as in Lemma <a href="#lem:exceptional-gradient" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:exceptional-gradient">5</a>. If $d>0$, put $W(x)=V(x)+(b/d)\cdot x$. An upper test $\psi$ for $W$ corresponds to the upper test $\chi=\psi-(b/d)\cdot x$ for $V$, and $b+d\nabla\chi=d\nabla\psi$. Thus $W$ satisfies the hypothesis of Lemma <a href="#lem:exceptional-gradient" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:exceptional-gradient">5</a>. It is subharmonic, and subtracting the affine function shows that $V$ is subharmonic too. ◻

</details>

The next estimate is a fixed-disk form of quantitative unique continuation. For the classical doubling estimate of Donnelly and Fefferman and the corresponding propagation bound, see <a id="pm-cite-13"></a>[[NazarovPolterovichSodin2005](#ref-NazarovPolterovichSodin2005), Theorem 4.1 and Lemma 4.14]. We give a proof using logarithmic profiles, which also supplies the local nonvanishing needed below.

<a id="stmt-prop-initial-growth"></a>
<div id="prop:initial-growth" class="proposition">

<a id="eq:initial-growth"></a>
**Proposition 6** (Initial growth on a fixed disk). *Let $(M,g)$ be a fixed smooth closed connected surface, and let $G\subset M$ be a fixed nonempty open coordinate disk. There is a finite constant $C=C(M,g,G)$ such that every nonzero real eigenfunction $-\Delta_g u=k^2u$ with $k>0$ satisfies $\|u\|_{L^2(G)}>0$ and $$\label{eq:initial-growth}
 \log\frac{\|u\|_{L^2(M)}}{\|u\|_{L^2(G)}}\le Ck.$$ Both norms use Riemannian volume. The constant is uniform over all eigenfunctions in every eigenspace.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-prop-initial-growth"><summary>Proof of stmt-prop-initial-growth</summary>

*Proof.* The positive spectral gap gives $k\ge k_1>0$. Interpret the log ratio in Equation <a href="#eq:initial-growth" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:initial-growth">[eq:initial-growth]</a> as $+\infty$ when the local norm is zero. If the assertion failed, we could choose eigenfunctions $u_j$, normalized by $\|u_j\|_{L^2(M)}=1$, such that their log ratios $R_j$ obey $R_j\ge j^2 k_j$. This also covers a zero local norm by repeating that eigenfunction. Set $S_j=j k_j$. Then $S_j\to\infty$ and $S_j/k_j\to\infty$, whether or not $k_j$ tends to infinity.

Apply Lemma <a href="#lem:profile-extraction" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:profile-extraction">2</a> on $M$ to the probability measures $|u_j|^2\,d\operatorname{vol}_g$. Compactness of $M$ and the compact upper bound with $F=M$ give $0\le\sup_M V\le0$. Upper semicontinuity ensures that this maximum is attained.

In a relatively compact isothermal chart, $g=p(x)(dx_1^2+dx_2^2)$ with $p>0$ smooth, and the coordinate functions satisfy $\Delta U_j+k_j^2pU_j=0$. The positive volume factor $p$ is bounded above and below on this chart. Multiplying $|U_j|^2\,dx$ by a fixed positive constant if necessary makes its mass at most one; the resulting logarithmic profile is the restriction of $V$. Theorem <a href="#thm:subharmonic-profile" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:subharmonic-profile">3</a> applies with $B_j=0$, $K_j=k_j$, and $D_j=S_j$, since $$\frac{k_j}{S_j}=\frac1j\longrightarrow0,
 \qquad
 \frac{k_j^2\|\nabla p\|_\infty}{S_j^2}
       =\frac{\|\nabla p\|_\infty}{j^2}\longrightarrow0.$$ Thus $V$ is subharmonic in every such chart. The strong maximum principle makes the set where $V=0$ open; it is also closed by upper semicontinuity and nonempty by the preceding maximum argument. Connectedness gives $V\equiv0$ on $M$.

On the other hand, the normalized mass of the given disk satisfies $$\frac{1}{2S_j}\log\int_G |u_j|^2\,d\operatorname{vol}_g
   =-\frac{R_j}{S_j}\le-j,$$ with the same conclusion when the integral is zero. This contradicts the open lower bound in Equation <a href="#eq:profile-bounds" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:profile-bounds">[eq:profile-bounds]</a>, whose right-hand side is $\sup_GV=0$. The argument proves both positivity of the local norm and Equation <a href="#eq:initial-growth" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:initial-growth">[eq:initial-growth]</a>. It uses only local isothermal charts and therefore imposes no orientability assumption. ◻

</details>

<a id="sec:subdivision"></a>
### Affine improvement under subdivision

The subharmonic profiles of Section <a href="#sec:profiles" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:profiles">2</a> admit a useful local affine correction. Averaged over a sufficiently fine square grid, the remaining growth decreases faster than the side length. We first establish this fact for profiles and then transfer it to eigenfunctions.

<div class="samepage">

<a id="stmt-def-growth"></a>
<div id="def:growth" class="definition">

<a id="eq:growth"></a>
**Definition 7** (Growth and affine excess). Fix an isothermal coordinate region in which $\Delta u+k^2p u=0$, and work in a relatively compact subset on which $p$ and $\nabla p$ have fixed uniform bounds. Set $R=1000$. Choose a root square $Q_0$, with side $s_0\le1$ and center $x_{Q_0}$, such that $\overline{\mathbb D_{2Rs_0}(x_{Q_0})}$ lies in this subset. For an integer $A\ge2$, subdivide each square into $A^2$ congruent squares and write $q\prec Q$ when $q$ is an immediate child of $Q$. For a square $Q$ of side $s=s(Q)$ and center $x_Q$, put $K_Q=ks$. For a real vector $b\in\mathbb R^2$, define $$\begin{aligned}
 E(Q,b)
 &=\log
 \frac{\norm{e^{-kb\cdot(x-x_Q)}u}_{L^2(\mathbb D_{Rs}(x_Q))}}
      {\norm{e^{-kb\cdot(x-x_Q)}u}_{L^2(\mathbb D_s(x_Q))}},\\
 m(Q,b)&=\frac{E(Q,b)}{K_Q},
 \qquad N(Q)=m(Q,0).
\end{aligned}
\label{eq:growth}$$ All planar norms here and below are unnormalized Euclidean norms. An average over children assigns weight $A^{-2}$ to each child.

</div>

</div>

A square $Q$ is called *admissible* if $s(Q)\le1$ and $\overline{\mathbb D_{Rs(Q)}(x_Q)}$ lies in the fixed coordinate region where the coefficient bounds hold. All descendants of the chosen root are admissible. The stronger $2R$ margin at the root reserves room for nearby squares outside its subdivision tree.

<a id="eq:untilted-comparison"></a>
Proposition <a href="#prop:initial-growth" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:initial-growth">6</a> ensures that the local norms in <a href="#eq:growth" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:growth">[eq:growth]</a> are nonzero. In particular, $E$, $m$, and $N$ are finite and nonnegative, since the disks are nested. Comparing the exponential weight on the two disks gives $$N(Q)\le m(Q,b)+(R+1)\abs{b}.
\label{eq:untilted-comparison}$$ Indeed the outer untilted norm is at most $e^{KR\abs b}$ times its tilted norm, whereas the inner untilted norm is at least $e^{-K\abs b}$ times its tilted norm, where $K=K_Q$. The same definitions and estimates apply to finite subtrees of an extended translated grid consisting of admissible squares.

The normalization by $K_Q$ explains why subdivision alone is insufficient. Put $\rho=A^{-1}$. An affine profile with slope $c\ne0$ has a difference of suprema equal to $(R-1)\rho|c|$ between concentric disks of radii $R\rho$ and $\rho$. Division by the child frequency $K_q=\rho K_Q$ cancels this first-order gain. We will subtract the local affine part to obtain an average profile excess of order $\rho^2(1+|\log\rho|)$, while keeping the average correction slope bounded. The latter bound controls the cost of changing weights through successive subdivisions.
